While dialing in the five constants above, `cardAnimTuning.ts` was a module-level mutable store (module vars + a listener `Set` + `useSyncExternalStore`) shaped exactly like `RecommendationsBody.tsx`'s own pre-existing empty-state headline shuffle-bag — a proven pattern for "one piece of state that isn't React state but still needs every subscriber to re-render on change." `CardAnimDebugPanel.tsx` was the tweak UI, rendered from one call site gated by a `SHOW_CARD_ANIM_DEBUG` flag in `RecommendationsBody.tsx`, letting duration/stagger/distance/both easing curves be retuned live in the browser with no rebuild.

Once the values above were settled, the panel, its store plumbing (`useCardAnimTuning`/`getCardAnimTuning`/`setCardAnimTuning`/`resetCardAnimTuning`, the `CardAnimTuning` interface, the listener `Set`), and `CardAnimDebugPanel.tsx` itself were all deleted — `cardAnimTuning.ts` now exports the five values as plain top-level constants (`CARD_ANIM_DURATION_MS`/`CARD_ANIM_STAGGER_MS`/`CARD_ANIM_DISTANCE_PX`/`CARD_ANIM_EASE_IN`/`CARD_ANIM_EASE_OUT`), matching `ROLL_DURATION_MS` (`RollingLabel.tsx`)/`EDIT_PANEL_TRANSITION_MS` (`MixerBar.tsx`)'s own convention exactly, rather than leaving the live-store machinery in place unused. `getEffectiveStaggerMs`/`getVisibleCardTierCount`/`getAnimatingPaceCount` are untouched — those were never part of the debug-tunability, just plain derived math that both `RecommendationsBody.tsx` and `ResultCard.tsx` still call directly. Duration/delay/timing-function/`--card-anim-distance` stay inline `style` on the animated element (not Tailwind classes) even now that the values are fixed constants — `animationDelay` alongside them is still genuinely per-card/dynamic (index-based), and a template-interpolated Tailwind class is invisible to its static scanner regardless (the same constraint `IconButton.tsx`'s `BORDER_CLASSES`/`MixerBar.tsx`'s `EDIT_PANEL_TRANSITION_MS` document), so there was no benefit to splitting the now-fixed values out to a separate static class.

### Mini error-hint stars — built, tried live, then deliberately removed

A `MiniStar` component briefly existed, decorating a handful of specific targets while a mixer error was active (a MatchSelector segment, RANDOM ×3, CLEAR SLOTS, the weakest filled tag slot) with a small rotating star anchored to specific text within each. After trying it live, the call was that the error subtext alone communicates clearly enough, and the extra spinning accents read as distracting rather than helpful — removed entirely (`MiniStar.tsx` deleted, `starred`/`starMaxValue` props and `weakestFilledTag` derivation all pulled back out of `TagSlot`/`MatchSelector`/`MixerSection`/`useKonomi`). Noting this so it doesn't get quietly rebuilt from scratch later: if highlighting/hinting comes up again, it's an open question worth a fresh design pass, not necessarily "bring back the mini stars" — try a different mechanism first (e.g. something less animated) rather than assuming this exact approach was just under-tuned.

**What stayed: `--primary-deep`, the new color token this feature introduced.** A bold, high-contrast variant of `--primary`, deliberately *not* derived the same way in every theme — for the two light-surface themes (default, "third"/Newsprint) it's `--primary` darkened, but for "dark"/Tide, whose surface is already dark and whose `--ink` is near-white, darkening further would move *toward* the background rather than away from it, so Tide's variant is `--primary` lightened by the same blend ratio instead. Still used for the mixer error subtext (`RecommendButton`'s `ERROR_MESSAGE` span) — that's the part of this feature that earned its keep. Defined in `ThemeColors` (`types.ts`), `THEMES` (`themes.ts`), and all three `[data-theme]` blocks in `globals.css`, the same three-places-in-sync pattern every other role-based color already follows.

## React Native portability

`src/lib/konomi/` (types, data, tokens, themes, scoring, and the `useKonomi` state hook) contains zero DOM or web-only APIs — no `document`, `window`, `HTMLElement`, or browser event types. It's plain TypeScript plus React hooks, which run the same way under React Native. The one deliberately web-only concern — HTML5 drag-and-drop — lives entirely in the web components (`TagChip`, `TagSlot`), which call into the same plain actions (`toggleTag`, `placeInSlot`, etc.) a React Native gesture handler would call.

## `TagCountBadge`: a tag-icon-shaped score badge, replacing a plain rotated rectangle

`ResultCard`'s score badge (`"2/3"`, or `"PERFECT"` on a full match) used to be a plain CSS rectangle: `border` + `bg` + `shadow-[var(--elev-1)]` + `-rotate-[4deg]`. It's now `TagCountBadge` (`src/components/konomi/TagCountBadge.tsx`), a rounded-rectangle body with a gently rounded point, echoing this app's own Tabler tag icon, plus a small transparent hole punched near the tip. Built and iterated entirely in a temporary, disposable preview file (`BadgeDesignPreview.tsx`, mounted standalone below `MixerBar` for side-by-side comparison, then deleted once a direction was chosen) before being extracted into this permanent component and wired into `ResultCard`.

**Body and point are one continuous SVG path, not two elements glued together at a seam. That's the load-bearing decision the rest of this rests on.** Earlier attempts built the point as a genuinely separate element (a CSS-bordered `<div>` for the body, a second shape for the point, aligned edge-to-edge), a shape which, no matter how precisely its geometry was matched to the body's own, could never be guaranteed to blend with zero sub-pixel seam artifacts, because the two are independently rasterized paint operations. A single `<path>` has no seam to blend, by construction, and this is what actually made a clean join possible. Every seam-alignment bug hit along the way (see below) traces back to a version that hadn't made this switch yet.

**The point's silhouette is the right half of a rotated square, matching the reference tag icon's own 45deg diagonals.** A square's four corners map to a diamond's top/right/bottom/left vertices under a 45deg rotation, so building the point this way isn't a stylistic choice, it's what makes the diagonal edges land at exactly 45deg for free. Rounding a 90deg corner with radius `r` pulls the outline back from the sharp corner by `r*(√2−1)` along that corner's own bisector (a plain consequence of a circle inscribed `r` away from each of the two edges meeting there). Left uncorrected, that recession leaves the point's rounded top/bottom corners short of the body's own full height, so `buildTagPath` inflates the point's sharp-corner half-diagonal by exactly that recession amount before rounding, landing the rounded corner's own outer edge exactly on the target height instead of falling short of it.

That fix has a second, unplanned benefit, confirmed on paper rather than just hoped for: at the exact point the rounded corner meets the body's own flat edge, its tangent direction works out to be purely horizontal (the corner's own bisector, rotated into the final frame, is vertical, and a rounding arc's tangent at its bisector point is perpendicular to that bisector). That's what makes the point flow into the body with no directional kink at the seam, not just a touching point: `buildTagPath` only ever rounds the two corners that actually need it (the seam transition and the tip), never the hidden corner a full rotated-square-with-uniform-radius construction would also round; a separate, abandoned exploration that *did* round all four uniformly hit exactly the seam-mismatch this avoids, which is part of why that construction was dropped in favor of this one.

**Text centers against a calculated "effective body edge," not the x-coordinate where the straight edge literally stops.** Centering directly against that literal cutoff measured out technically correct but visually wrong: reported live as "the text looks shifted left" even after confirming (via a debug overlay, and independently via the live path data) that it really was centered against that reference point. The curve just past the straight edge is gentle, and a real span of it still reads as part of "the body," not "the point." The fix defines the edge as the x-coordinate where the shape's own height has narrowed to a fixed fraction of full height (`BODY_HEIGHT_FRACTION`, currently 0.85) rather than a fixed pixel offset past the straight-edge cutoff, so it stays correct if the point's own radius or the badge's height ever change, instead of needing a re-tuned magic number each time.

**An SVG `stroke` centers on the path (half inside, half outside), unlike a CSS `border`, which is entirely inset.** Making the SVG stroke behave like the inset CSS borders every other bordered element in this app uses: the path is built at `hEff = HEIGHT - borderWidth`, then the whole thing is translated by `borderWidth/2`, so the stroke's outer edge lands exactly on the badge's own declared bounds regardless of border width, the same border-box guarantee CSS gives a bordered `<div>` for free.

**The hole is a true SVG `<mask>` cutout with its own independent-width ring, not baked into the outline path.** An earlier version added the hole as a second circular subpath in the same path string, combined with `fillRule="evenodd"`, which worked correctly for the fill (confirmed live: the "no border" treatment showed a clean cutout with the real background visible through it), but a single `<path>` element has exactly one `stroke-width`, so the hole's own ring was forced to match the main outline's border width exactly. Asked to make the ring thinner than the main border, that's structurally impossible for one path, so the hole is a `<mask>` (a true cutout to whatever's actually behind the badge, which matters for how the "bordered + shadow" treatment interacts with it, below) applied to a `<g>` wrapping the plain outline path, with a separate `<circle>` drawn afterward purely for the ring's own, independently-sized stroke.

**`drop-shadow` interacting with a true alpha-channel hole is physically correct, not a bug, but only reads as "see-through" if the shadow's own offset is small relative to the hole.** `drop-shadow` computes its shadow from the element's own rendered alpha (hole included), then paints a solid-color copy of that exact silhouette (hole and all) offset by a fixed amount underneath the original. Background shows through only where the true hole and the shadow's own *shifted* hole overlap. At the hole's original size this overlap was a clearly visible crescent at the default `--elev-1` offset (2px); once the hole's radius was cut down (see below), that same 2px offset became large enough relative to the smaller hole to cover it almost entirely, reading as a solid dot with no visible crescent at all. Confirmed live at 16x zoom, not assumed. The shadow offset (currently 1px, not `--elev-1`'s literal 2px) has to stay small relative to whatever the hole's own radius is for the see-through effect to actually read.

**The hole's radius is a fraction of `HEIGHT` (`HOLE_RADIUS_RATIO`), not a fixed pixel value, because `HEIGHT` itself is measurement-driven and can legitimately change for reasons that have nothing to do with the hole.** `HEIGHT` is derived from the label's own measured line-height plus vertical padding plus border (the same "measure content, add padding/border" construction a plain CSS box gets for free), so it isn't a fixed number tuned once, it recomputes on every render. A hole radius left as a fixed pixel value went stale exactly once already: tightening the label's line-height (below) shrank `HEIGHT`, and a hole that had stayed fixed at its original radius immediately read as too big for the new, shorter badge. Deriving it as a ratio of whatever `HEIGHT` actually is keeps it right-sized automatically.

**The hole's position is derived directly from the same `bodyBoxLeft`/`bodyBoxWidth` values that define the text-centering box, not recomputed independently.** An earlier version computed the hole's x-coordinate with its own separate expression that was subtly inconsistent with a later fix to how the body box's own left edge was anchored, and drifted exactly `borderWidth/2` off from the body box's true right edge as a result. Confirmed live via `getBBox()` (exact SVG-space geometry, not CSS-pixel-rounded `boundingBox()`, which showed enough measurement noise to obscure the real bug at first). Reusing the same values outright, rather than re-deriving an equivalent expression, means the two can't drift apart again. The default hole position is nudged 1px left of dead-center on that edge (`HOLE_NUDGE_X`). Confirmed dead-center itself measures out perfectly true first, so the nudge is a deliberate stylistic choice, not a correction for a misalignment.

**A font's default line-height reserves descender space (for letters like g/y/p/q/j) that digit labels like `"2/3"` or `"PERFECT"` never use. Centering on the CSS line-height box is not the same as centering the visible glyphs.** The label span's `top: 50%` + `translateY(-50%)` pair measurably centers its own line-height box exactly on the badge's true center (confirmed via the resolved transform matrix), but the glyphs themselves still read as sitting above center, because the reserved space above and below them isn't symmetric even though the box is. `leading-none` on the label tightens the line-height enough that the box hugs the actual glyph shapes closely, bringing the glyphs' own visual center much closer to true center. Confirmed live, zoomed in, before and after.

**Settled defaults**, all independently tunable, none a fixed preset: `bordered`/`shadow`/`bold` are independent booleans (any combination valid, all defaulting to match the very first version of this component: bordered, shadowed, regular weight). `tiltDeg` defaults to `0` (`ResultCard`'s own call site passes `-5`, a touch more than its previous plain rectangle's own `-rotate-[4deg]`, tuned once placed alongside this shape). The component itself stays neutral, since a rotation default belongs to a specific placement, not the shape.

### Placement: top-right of the tag scroll area, mirroring `RatingBadge`'s own corner on the cover

`TagCountBadge` originally stacked above the title (bottom-left of the cover, same box as the title/meta). It now sits at the top-right of the tag area below the cover instead, overlapping the top row of tag chips when the row is full. Two badges, each anchored to a corner of its own half of the card: this one close to the tags it's counting, `RatingBadge` at the bottom-right of the cover above (a corner of the artwork, not tied to anything specific on it).

**The overlap with tag chips is intentional, not an oversight.** The tighter association with what the badge is actually counting (the visible/matched tags right below it) was judged to outweigh the cost of sometimes covering a chip. This needed an explicit `z-[3]` on the badge's own wrapper: one tier above `TagScrollArea`'s own `z-[2]`, for the same reason that div needed an explicit z-index over the scrim/veil beneath it elsewhere in this file. A `z-index:auto` sibling doesn't reliably paint over an explicit-z one regardless of DOM order.

**The two badges' insets are independently tuned, not matched exactly, despite the "mirrored pair" framing.** `RatingBadge` sits at a fixed `right-[24px]`/`bottom-[22px]`. Matching those values exactly for `TagCountBadge` does not visually center- or edge-align the two badges with each other: `RatingBadge` is a plain rectangle, while `TagCountBadge`'s own point extends further right than its text and padding alone would (see `totalW`'s own comment below). The current values (`top-[20px]`/`right-[17px]`) were chosen by eye against a live comparison, not derived from `RatingBadge`'s own insets or from a center-alignment calculation.

**A real sizing bug surfaced once the two badges were actually compared side by side: `totalW`'s own "generous" margin was much bigger than its own comment claimed.** `totalW` (the SVG wrapper's declared width) used to be `bodyW + hEff/2 + POINT_RADIUS*2 + borderWidth*2`, commented as "deliberately generous, not pixel-exact, erring larger just adds a hair of transparent margin." Measured live: that "hair" was 18px out of roughly 55px total width. Because `TagCountBadge`'s wrapper was positioned with an exact CSS inset specifically to match `RatingBadge`'s own, that extra invisible margin made the badge visibly sit much further from the card's edge than `RatingBadge` does, even though both wrappers landed at the identical inset. The true visible tip (stroke included) works out to exactly `bodyW + hEff/2`: the rounded tip's own peak lands at `bodyWEff + hEff/2` in the path's own local frame, and the shared translate plus the stroke's own outward half both add `borderWidth/2`, which cancels out against the `borderWidth` already subtracted out of `bodyWEff`. `totalW` is now that exact value plus a 2px buffer, just enough to cover the shadow filter's own small rightward bleed when `shadow` is on.

## `ResultCard`'s back face: real description + streaming services, replacing dummy data

The back face used to be only a "WATCH ON" header plus a full-height stacked list from `streamsFor()` (`src/lib/konomi/scoring.ts`, now deleted), a deterministic-per-title pick from a hardcoded 7-platform list (`PLATFORMS` in `src/lib/konomi/data.ts`, also deleted). That file's own comment explained why: "the approved Supabase schema has no platforms table." There was no series description anywhere in the app either.

Both are now real, sourced from AniList's own `Media.description` and `Media.externalLinks` (filtered to `type: STREAMING`), confirmed live against AniList's actual GraphQL schema before building anything, not assumed from training data. Two new columns on `series` (`supabase/migrations/0007_add_series_description.sql`, `0008_add_series_streaming_services.sql`), `description text` and `streaming_services jsonb not null default '[]'`. `jsonb`, not `text[]` the way `genres` is: unlike genres, each streaming entry needs per-value metadata (the watch url), the same reason tags needed `rank` on a join row rather than living in a plain array, but a full join table (mirroring `tags`/`series_tags`) isn't warranted since the platform list is a small, fixed, curated set with no per-platform aggregate stat this app needs anywhere.

**The streaming list is a curated allow-list, not everything AniList's `externalLinks` returns.** Sampled live: popular series' `externalLinks` include real platforms (Crunchyroll, Netflix, Hulu, Prime Video, Max, Disney Plus, HIDIVE) alongside noise that isn't a real "watch the whole series here" option for this app's audience (YouTube promo-only playlists, regional platforms like iQiyi/WeTV/Bilibili). `STREAMING_ALLOW_LIST` in `scripts/import-anilist.ts` is a hand-maintained `Record<string, string>` keyed by AniList's own exact `site` string, valued by the display name to store, the same "retune by hand" precedent already used for `--masthead-height`/`--mixer-bar-height` in `globals.css`. It also absorbs real naming mismatches found live (AniList's `"Amazon Prime Video"`/`"Disney Plus"` vs. this app's shorter `"Prime Video"`/`"Disney+"`).

**The description is stored pre-cleaned, at import time, as plain text, never as raw HTML.** AniList's `description(asHtml: false)` still contains literal `<br><br>` tags in practice (confirmed live, not just documented), and often a trailing `(Source: Crunchyroll)`-style citation, and can carry AniList's own `~!spoiler!~` markup. `cleanDescription()` in `scripts/import-anilist.ts` strips the citation, strips spoiler-wrapped text entirely (not just the delimiters, this is a short teaser summary, not a plot-details view), and converts literal `<br>` tags to real `\n` newlines, rendered client-side with CSS `white-space: pre-line`. Deliberately never `dangerouslySetInnerHTML`: this is ultimately third-party content, and there's no rendering need that justifies opening an XSS surface for it.

**Flip-vs-link-click reuses the exact pattern already in this file, not a new one.** The whole card flips on a plain `onClick={() => onFlip(series.title)}` on the root `<article>`, so any unstopped click bubbles up and flips it. The lock and swap/dice icons already solve this the same one-line way, `e.stopPropagation()` as the first statement in their own `onClick` (never `preventDefault`, which would also block the element's own default behavior). The streaming `<a target="_blank">` tags get the identical guard, confirmed live (not just reasoned): clicked a real streaming link, captured the resulting `popup`'s own URL (a real AniList-sourced watch page), and confirmed the card's own back-face opacity read `"1"` (still showing, not flipped back to FRONT) both immediately before and after the click.

**Two real, non-obvious CSS Grid bugs, both confirmed live via computed styles before landing on the fix that actually works, worth remembering as a pair.** The description panel needs to fill whatever space the (variable-height, sometimes-absent) streaming row below it doesn't use, the same "one side fixed, one side absorbs the rest" shape the cover/tag-area split above already solves. That split's own `flex-1 + min-h-0` technique needs to apply directly to the element that has to stretch, though, and here that element is `ScrollFadeArea`'s own root `<div>`, whose className is entirely internal to `ScrollFadeArea.tsx`, not exposed as a prop to style from a caller.

CSS Grid's default `stretch` behavior (unlike flexbox, which only stretches on the cross axis by default, grid stretches items on *both* axes) looked like a clean way around needing to touch that root at all, since a grid item gets full free sizing from its own track with zero className injected onto it. It took two attempts to actually land it, though, both wrong in different, specific ways:

1. **First attempt: only the outer container (`grid grid-rows-[1fr_auto]`) was a grid; the panel wrapper directly around `ScrollFadeArea` was a plain block div.** Stretch only reaches *direct* grid items: the wrapper (a direct item of the outer grid) stretched correctly, but `ScrollFadeArea`'s root, one level further in as the wrapper's own child, was back to being an ordinary auto-height block, inheriting nothing. Confirmed live: content silently hard-clipped by the wrapper's own `overflow-hidden`, no scrollbar, no fade, since `ScrollFadeArea` itself never received a height smaller than its own content in the first place.
2. **Second attempt: made the wrapper `grid` too, but with no explicit `grid-template-rows` at all.** An *implicit* grid track (created by content alone, sized via `grid-auto-rows`, default `auto`) sizes itself to fit its content, exactly like a plain block does. It doesn't fill the container the way an explicit `1fr` track does. Adding an explicit `grid-rows-[1fr]` still wasn't enough on its own, either: a `1fr` track's own automatic minimum size is content-based by default, the same "won't shrink below content" behavior `min-h-0` exists to override on a flex/grid *item*, except the item sizing this particular track is `ScrollFadeArea`'s root, which can't take a `min-h-0` className from here any more than it could take `flex-1`. Confirmed live via `getComputedStyle` at each level of the chain: the wrapper's own resolved height was correct (matching its `1fr` share of the *outer* grid), but its internal `gridTemplateRows` value resolved to something noticeably *taller* than that, meaning the row grew to fit content instead of capping it, and the visible symptom was identical to attempt 1, silent hard-clipping, no scroll affordance.

**The actual fix: `grid-rows-[minmax(0,1fr)]` on the wrapper, not a bare `1fr`.** `minmax(0, 1fr)` moves the "allow shrinking below content" override from the *item* (unreachable here) to the *track* itself, which is the one place this wrapper's own className can still reach. `0` is the track's explicit minimum, `1fr` is its share of genuinely available space. Confirmed live via the same `getComputedStyle` chain: `ScrollFadeArea`'s root and its scroll viewport both now resolve to the wrapper's real, capped height, `hasOverflow` correctly flips to `true` on long descriptions, and the fade gradient's own DOM element (confirmed present, not just assumed, `linear-gradient(to top, var(--surface), transparent)` at the correct 18px/bottom-0 position) renders at the right place, just genuinely subtle at that size, the same intentionally-soft depth cue `TagScrollArea`'s own fade already is, not a bug.

**The generalized lesson, worth remembering the next time a component's internal root needs to stretch to fill a caller-controlled area: grid's free, no-className stretch only propagates one level at a time, and only past the automatic-minimum-size floor if the *track*, not just the item, is told it's allowed to shrink.** Wrapping a black-box component (no `className`/style passthrough on its own root) in a *plain* `grid` div is not sufficient by itself, and neither is adding a bare `1fr` row; the reachable element (the wrapper) has to be a grid *at every level between it and the part that needs to stretch*, and the specific track sizing the un-reachable root needs `minmax(0, 1fr)`, not `1fr`, or its own automatic content-based minimum wins regardless of how the level above it resolved.

### `cleanDescription` was an allow-list of one tag, not a general strip, confirmed live on real cards before the fix

The first version only ever handled `<br>` (converting it to a real newline). Every *other* inline tag AniList's `description` field can include, `<i>`, `<b>`, `<a href="...">`, and so on, passed straight through untouched, since there was never a general "strip anything that looks like a tag" pass, only a specific one for `<br>`. Reported live against a real card (`"The final season of <i>Fairy Tail</i>"` rendering with the literal `<i>`/`</i>` visible), confirmed by reading the function directly rather than guessing: no other tag pattern existed anywhere in it. Not a stale-data symptom either, upserting on `anilist_id` means a re-run fully overwrites every row, so the bug was in the cleaning logic itself, not in data left over from before cleaning existed.

Fixed with a genuinely general pass, `text.replace(/<[^>]+>/g, "")`, added *after* the `<br>` to `\n` conversion (order matters: the general strip removes tag markup with no replacement at all, so running it first would silently delete `<br>` too, losing the line break instead of converting it) and *before* the trailing `"(Source: ...)"` citation strip (in case that citation is ever itself tag-wrapped). Verified two ways, not just one: a standalone regex test against the exact reported string, and a full re-import followed by scanning all 500 stored descriptions for `/<[^>]+>/` matches, zero remaining, including the specific Fairy Tail row that surfaced the bug.

### The back face's two zones now reuse FRONT's own cover/tag-area height tokens directly, not an independent split

Originally `grid-rows-[1fr_auto]`: the description took whatever the streaming row's own natural content height left over. That's a fundamentally different sizing rule than FRONT's cover/tag-area split (`--result-card-cover-height`/`--result-card-tag-area-height`, both in `globals.css`, see "Recommendations layout" above), so flipping a card jumped between two unrelated proportions instead of reading as the same layout rotating in place. Changed on request to reuse those exact same two tokens for BACK's own two zones, so the boundary between them lands at the identical Y position FRONT's cover/tag-area boundary does.

**The header can't be an extra flow sibling stacked above the two zones, or the total height breaks the very match this exists for.** `--result-card-cover-height` + `--result-card-tag-area-height` already sum to exactly `--result-card-height` minus the card's own top/bottom border, the same budget FRONT's cover-box and tag-wrapper split between them with nothing left over. Adding a header as a third flow item above both zones would push the total past that budget by the header's own height. Instead, the "WATCH ON" header lives *inside* zone 1 (the `--result-card-cover-height`-tall zone), splitting that zone's own space between the header (its natural size) and the description (fills whatever's left), the same "header overlaid within the cover-height zone" relationship FRONT's own icon-row/title already have with cover-box, just via normal flow (`grid-rows-[auto_minmax(0,1fr)]`) instead of absolute positioning, since a header and prose description would visually collide if stacked as overlapping layers the way FRONT's icon-row sits over a plain photo.

**This adds a third level to the same nested-grid-stretch chain documented above, mechanically, not a new technique.** Reaching `ScrollFadeArea`'s root from zone 1's own `h-[var(--result-card-cover-height)]` now goes: zone 1 (`grid-rows-[auto_minmax(0,1fr)]`) β†’ a padded wrapper for row 2 (`min-h-0`, its own `grid-rows-[minmax(0,1fr)]`, needed so the header stays full-bleed, edge to edge, with padding scoped to only the description below it) β†’ the `bg-surface` panel (same `grid-rows-[minmax(0,1fr)]` + `min-h-0` pairing as before) β†’ `ScrollFadeArea`. Each level pairs `min-h-0` (the item's own override, for its role as *its parent's* item) with `grid-rows-[minmax(0,1fr)]` (the track's own override, for *its own* child) rather than trying to prove which one is strictly necessary at each level in isolation, the combination was already confirmed working end to end for the original two-level case above, and re-deriving from scratch whether one half is skippable at a third level isn't worth the risk of quietly reintroducing the exact silent-hard-clip bug that whole section exists to document.

**Zone 2 (streaming) mirrors tag-wrapper's own `flex-1 min-h-0 h-[var(--result-card-tag-area-height)]` treatment directly, but doesn't reach for `ScrollFadeArea` a second time.** Up to 7 allow-listed platforms (`STREAMING_ALLOW_LIST`) wrapped inside a zone as short as this token's own 63px floor can genuinely need more vertical room than fits, but there's no row-snap/peek grid here the way `TagScrollArea` has, just a plain `flex-wrap` row of chips, so a native `overflow-y-auto` is enough, no fade/arrow treatment, no third `ScrollFadeArea` instance, no third round of the grid-stretch problem to solve for a case that doesn't need it.

**No border between the two zones on this face, unlike cover-box's own `border-b` on FRONT, confirmed as a deliberate choice against the live result, not an oversight.** The proportional match itself, confirmed live via `getBoundingClientRect()` at four viewport heights spanning `--result-card-height`'s own full clamp range (700-1400px tall), landed at exactly 0.00px difference between FRONT's cover/tag-area boundary and BACK's own zone 1/zone 2 boundary at every one of them. A visible divider line at that same boundary read as unwanted clutter once seen against the actual result, the `bg-surface` description panel already gives the description its own clear edge without needing a second line directly below it.

### Streaming chips: AniList's own icon and color, rendered as a flat colored-silhouette mask, not a plain `<img>`

AniList's `externalLinks` also carries `icon` (a transparent 64x64 PNG on their own CDN) and `color` (the platform's own brand hex) per link, alongside `site`/`url`/`type`. Both confirmed live, the same way `url` already was: sampled 50 popular series, every allow-listed platform (`STREAMING_ALLOW_LIST`) had exactly one consistent, non-null value for each, the icon URL itself even encoding AniList's own fixed per-site id (e.g. the `5-` in Crunchyroll's own path). Both pulled straight through `streamServicesFor()` alongside `url`, not hardcoded the way the display name is: unlike the name (which genuinely needs a manual override in a few cases, `"Amazon Prime Video"` to `"Prime Video"`), icon and color are already exactly what this app wants as-is.

**A plain `<img src={s.icon}>` was tried first and confirmed live to look broken, not just suboptimal.** Every one of AniList's icons turned out to be a white (or very pale) glyph on a transparent background, meant to sit on that platform's own dark brand color elsewhere, not this chip's light `--surface`. Rendered plain, Crunchyroll's mark, Netflix's `N`, Hulu's `h`, all five spot-checked platforms, were technically present (confirmed via `naturalWidth`/`complete`, a real loaded image, not a broken one) but nearly invisible at normal size, only recognizable once zoomed in far past any real reading distance.

**Fixed with a CSS mask, not a recolor filter or a re-hosted/reprocessed image.** The icon `<img>` became a plain `<span>` with `background-color: s.color` (falling back to `--ink`, this app's own neutral foreground token, on the rare null) and `mask-image: url(s.icon)` (plus the `-webkit-` prefixed pair of every mask property, unprefixed alone doesn't render on Safari). A mask image uses its source's own alpha channel as a stencil, wherever the PNG is opaque shows the element's `background-color` through, wherever it's transparent stays transparent, turning a white-on-transparent glyph into a flat silhouette in whatever color is behind it. This was chosen over the alternatives directly: a CSS `filter` (`hue-rotate`/`saturate`/etc.) can't reliably hit an *exact* target hex from a white source, only approximate it; a colored circle/badge behind the plain white icon works but reads as a heavier, more skeuomorphic treatment than this app's own flat aesthetic elsewhere; re-hosting a server-recolored image would need a new image-processing step at import time and lose the "always exactly what AniList currently serves" property the mask gets for free.

**Verified against all 7 allow-listed platforms individually, then again as a row together, not just one in isolation.** Each renders as a clean, immediately recognizable flat mark in its own real brand color, Crunchyroll's orange wolf/moon, Netflix's red `N`, Hulu's green `h`, Max's blue `m`, HIDIVE's cyan `HI`, Disney+'s purple `D`, Prime Video's orange smile-arrow `a`. A card with three chips in the same row (Prime Video/HIDIVE/Netflix) confirmed they read as one cohesive, designed set of marks rather than a jumble of pasted logos, each one distinct at a glance without needing its own label to identify it, the label text next to it is now reinforcing the mark, not the only legible part of the chip.

### Zone 1's header is the series title now, reusing `TagSlot`'s already-proven `line-clamp-2` + descender-padding pattern rather than inventing a new one

The "WATCH ON 配信" label (`font-display`, `uppercase`, a small `font-body` companion span) is gone. Zone 1's header bar now shows `series.title` itself, styled to match FRONT's own `<h3>` (`font-body font-black`, natural case, no tracking) rather than the old label's own arbitrary styling, since it's the same piece of real content now, not a fixed two-word tag.

**Wrap up to 2 lines, then truncate, is exactly what `line-clamp-2` already does, and `TagSlot.tsx` already solved the one real gotcha that comes with it.** Rather than re-deriving that from scratch, the header reuses the identical pattern that file already documents in detail: `line-clamp-2 break-words pb-[4px]`. `-webkit-line-clamp`'s own clip boundary sits at exactly 2 × line-height with zero allowance for a glyph descender (the tail on a "g"/"y"/"p"/"q"/"j"), which a tight `leading-tight` line-height doesn't leave room for on its own, confirmed by `TagSlot.tsx` living through exactly this bug already. `pb-[4px]` (not a line-height or line-count change) buys that room back at the padding box, where `line-clamp`'s own `overflow: hidden` actually clips, the same value already confirmed live there as the first one that stops clipping without over-padding.

**Verified live across three real title lengths, not just the two extremes.** A one-word title (`"InuYasha"`) stays on a single line. A ~25-character title (`"Food Wars! The Fifth Plate"`) wraps to exactly 2 lines with no ellipsis, confirming the common case doesn't get needlessly truncated. A real ~50-character title (`"JoJo's Bizarre Adventure: Diamond is Unbreakable"`) wraps to 2 lines and then truncates with a trailing ellipsis (`"...Diamond ..."`), confirmed via the same `scrollHeight`-vs-`clientHeight` check this file already uses elsewhere to detect real overflow (a genuine 21px gap here, not the few-px sub-pixel noise a false positive would show).

**One measurement false-positive along the way, worth remembering: `document.querySelector("h3")` (or an equivalent single, unscoped selector) finds FRONT's own title `<h3>`, not BACK's, since both exist in the DOM at once and FRONT's comes first in document order.** An early check against a bare `h3` selector reported *every* title as "truncated," including a plain one-word name, which is impossible. The bug wasn't in the clamp logic at all, it was measuring the wrong element (FRONT's own untruncated title, whose own small, constant `scrollHeight`/`clientHeight` gap turned out to just be ordinary single-line rounding, unrelated to clamping). Scoping the selector to the specific `line-clamp-2` header resolved it. The same class of mistake this file's own "grabbed the hidden face's scroller instead of the visible one" note already describes elsewhere, same fix each time: scope the query to the element that's actually being asked about, don't rely on document order picking the right one of several structurally-similar candidates.

## A `localhost`-only black flicker during window resize turned out to be Next.js's own dev-mode indicator, not app code

**Reported live: a quick black flicker across the whole browser (not just the page content) during rapid window resizing, seemingly horizontal-direction-specific (both narrowing and widening triggered it, neither vertical resize did), confirmed present on `localhost` and confirmed absent on the deployed production build.** Worth recording in detail, since "prod is clean, dev isn't" is exactly the kind of signal that's easy to misdiagnose as a real app bug and spend real time chasing in application code.

**Investigated as a code regression first, ruled out methodically before looking anywhere else.** At the time this was reported, the only uncommitted changes were the `MixerBar` Random-gap fix and the `--result-card-tag-area-height` floor raise (both documented elsewhere in this file), and neither touches anything resize-reactive or continuously animating. A live sweep across every `MixerBar` breakpoint this app defines (511 through 1071px), several passes back and forth, produced zero console errors/warnings and zero oscillating layout values (`scrollHeight`/`clientWidth` sampled twice, 150ms apart, at each width, with no further input in between, the same technique this file's own `--mixer-bar-height` reflow-loop entry already used to catch a *real* instance of this class of bug). That ruled out a genuine JS-level reflow loop in this app's own code.

**The actual cause: a `<nextjs-portal>` custom element with its own shadow root, confirmed present in the page's DOM.** This is Next.js's own development-mode indicator (a small floating badge, normally tucked in a corner, showing live compile/build status), rendered only in `next dev`, entirely absent from the production bundle. That single fact explains every symptom at once: present on `localhost`, absent in production (the indicator literally doesn't exist there), and unrelated to anything in this app's own component code, since it's a separate element Next.js itself injects. The "only horizontal, not vertical" detail is consistent with (though not independently proven here) the indicator's own internal positioning logic reacting to viewport *width* specifically.

**Left enabled, by request, rather than disabled via `devIndicators: false` in `next.config.ts`.** It's a pure local dev-tooling convenience (compile-status at a glance, no terminal-switching needed) with zero effect on the shipped app either way. If this flicker resurfaces and starts feeling disruptive during dev testing, the fix is that one config line, not application code. Don't go looking in `MixerBar`/`ResultCard`/`RollingLabel` for it again.

## MixerBar's edit panel: Row 2 (the controls row) and cardRef's own box both had to stop moving during resize and open/close — two related but distinct bugs, two distinct fixes

Reported directly, precisely, after the live-drag performance fix above landed: "the mixbar stays anchored fixed at the bottom of the page, and the panel grows smoothly upward from it — the mixbar base should not move at all. Current behavior: the whole mixbar pushes down as the panel goes up, and the panel appears instantly then fills." A separate, related report on the drag specifically: the grip's resize interaction was visually dragging the whole card (edit panel *and* the controls row below it) as one unit, when it should only ever resize the panel above a stationary controls row. Both trace back to the same category of bug the "Round 1/2/3" `layout="position"` history above already documents extensively (search this file for "Round 3") — a *faked* position transform standing in for real geometry that isn't actually changing smoothly — but they're two separate mechanisms (the live drag's hand-driven Motion values vs. the discrete open/close/snap case's `layout="position"`), so they needed two separate fixes.

### Bug 1: live drag — cardRef's own box was sliding as a rigid whole, not growing, because `dragCardTranslateY` tracked the wrong thing

The drag-performance fix (previous entry) pins `growableRef`/`dialogRef`'s real height to `poolPanelMaxHeight` for a drag's entire duration (one real layout write, not one per tick) and reveals the live target via a `clip-path` motion value. `cardRef` gets a matching `dragCardTranslateY` motion value to visually compensate for that real-height jump. This went through three attempts before landing on the correct one — worth recording all three, since the failure modes are easy to reintroduce by accident and each one looked locally plausible.

**Attempt 1 (the original design): `dragCardTranslateY = -(poolPanelMaxHeight - next)`, recomputed every tick to track the live drag target.** The intuitive choice — the panel visually needs to track the pointer, so surely the compensating transform should too. Row 2 (the tag slots + buttons row), a plain DOM *child* of `cardRef` sitting beside `growableRef`, inherited this transform for free via ordinary CSS transform inheritance and visibly moved during every resize.

**Attempt 2: gave Row 2 its own *separate*, per-tick motion value to cancel out cardRef's live delta, leaving cardRef's own formula untouched.** Reasoned (correctly, in isolation) that Row 2's needed compensation is the negative of how far `dragCardTranslateY` has moved *since drag start*, not a copy of its value — set to `0` at drag start, `startHeight - next` on every subsequent tick. Verified via `getComputedStyle` sampling through a live 100px drag: Row 2's own top stayed at exactly 483.2px the entire time (0.0px delta). This looked like a complete fix and was reported as one. **It wasn't.** Reported directly, immediately after, with a screen recording: "the resize manual drag/grip interaction is wrong, it completely moves the entire card and contents." Measuring `cardBottom` directly (not just Row 2) confirmed it: cardRef's own box — border, background, shadow — was sliding through the full drag distance (150px of cardBottom movement for a 150px drag), even though Row 2 measured as stationary. Attempt 2 had only ever corrected Row 2's own inherited *share* of cardRef's problem; cardRef's own box was still wrong, just with nothing left to visibly prove it apart from the border itself sliding underneath the now-stationary controls.

**Root cause, once actually traced through the formula's own math rather than assumed correct:** `growableRef`'s real height pins to `poolPanelMaxHeight` once, at drag start, and the outer wrapper's real `marginTop` stays frozen at whatever it was computed for `startHeight` (deliberately — recomputing it live during a drag was tried earlier in this same effort and reverted, see `effectiveHeightPx`'s own comment in `MixerBar.tsx`). That mismatch makes cardRef's real, untransformed box **constant** throughout the drag, wrong by a **fixed** amount (`poolPanelMaxHeight - startHeight`) that has nothing to do with `next`. Attempt 1's formula tracked `next` — a moving target chasing a fixed error, correct only at the single instant they coincidentally agree (`next == startHeight`, i.e. t=0) and increasingly wrong as the drag proceeds away from that point. This is exactly why cardBottom's drift scaled with drag distance.

**The actual fix: since the error is fixed, the correction has to be fixed too.** `dragCardTranslateY` is now `-(poolPanelMaxHeight - startHeight)`, computed once in `handleResizeStart` and never touched again until `handleEnd` resets it to `0`. This makes cardRef's own box — and therefore Row 2, and anything else riding inside it — perfectly stationary for the drag's entire duration, verified via the same `cardBottom` measurement that caught the bug (0.0px drift across the full range this time, not just at the two ends). Attempt 2's whole mechanism (Row 2's own separate compensator) became unnecessary and was removed entirely: it was only ever working around cardRef's own formula being wrong, and once cardRef stopped moving, Row 2 (with no transform of its own at all) is simply, structurally correct.

**The tradeoff this leaves, now the only thing actually animating during a live drag:** cardRef's own box is sized and positioned for its full `poolPanelMaxHeight` extent from the very first frame and stays there, unmoving, the whole time — only the clip-path-revealed content grows with the pointer. Checked this doesn't cause the revealed region to overshoot Row 2 at the top of the range either: cardRef's box is anchored so that a *fully* revealed panel lands exactly where the real, non-drag geometry would put it, and the reveal only ever grows toward that point for any reachable value of `next`, never past it.

**A second, unrelated bug surfaced during this same investigation, via the exact same "the entire card and contents" report: the resize panel went completely blank and unclickable after every drag.** `growableRef`'s `clip-path` is bound to a motion value (`dragClipPath`), inside a style object that used to be *conditionally* present — `dragHeightPx !== null ? { height, overflow, clipPath } : undefined`. React's own style reconciliation correctly removes plain values (confirmed: `height`/`overflow` reverted correctly every time) when a later render's style object stops mentioning them, but a motion-value-bound property doesn't get the same treatment — Motion keeps directly writing whatever the motion value currently holds to the DOM regardless of what the component's own JSX says on a later render. Confirmed directly: `growableRef`'s inline style read `clip-path: inset(0px 0px 310.297px)` — clipping away virtually the entire box — *after* a drag had fully ended and every other drag-only style had correctly reverted. That's why content vanished (the clipped region simply isn't painted) and why `Close`, every tab, and every chip became unclickable (a clip-path-hidden region drops out of hit-testing too, confirmed via `document.elementsFromPoint` finding an unrelated part of the page underneath). Fixed two ways together: `clipPath` is now unconditionally present in `growableRef`'s style object (matching the pattern `cardRef`'s own `style.y` already used, for the identical reason), and the value it resolves to when idle changed from `0` (which the clip formula turns into "hide everything") to `Number.POSITIVE_INFINITY` (which the same formula, `max(0, poolPanelMaxHeight - revealPx)`, turns into an explicit zero-inset — no clipping — regardless of `poolPanelMaxHeight`'s own current value, sidestepping a declaration-order constraint that ruled out using `poolPanelMaxHeight` directly here).

**A third, real but lower-priority gap found in the same pass, not fixed: content doesn't visually grow *during* an active drag, only catches up instantly on release.** `TagPoolBody`'s own `poolPanelHeightPx` prop reads `effectiveHeightPx`, which — like `targetMarginTop` — is frozen at `startHeight` for a drag's entire duration (the same freeze the performance fix depends on). So while `growableRef`'s clip-path window correctly grows with the pointer, `TagPoolBody`'s own internal chip-grid area does not: the window reveals more space, but there's no new content sized to fill it until the drag ends and `effectiveHeightPx` unfreezes. Visually this reads as the revealed region staying blank (or static) while dragging, then snapping to its correct, fully-populated state on release — confirmed this is where content ends up correctly (24 chips, tab strip, all clickable) immediately after release, so this is specifically a *during-the-gesture* gap, not a data or rendering bug. Left undone this round: fixing it means giving `TagPoolBody` a live-tracking height during a drag (a motion value, mirroring the pattern already established here), which means changing a file this session hadn't otherwise touched, with the verification budget better spent confirming the two bugs above (both directly reported, both now measured at 0.0px drift / correctly unconditional) than opening a third, unreported surface under time pressure.

### Bug 2: open/close/snap — `layout="position"` fakes a position transition on a box whose real size already snapped to final

This is the deeper of the two bugs, and it's why Round 3's `layout="position"` design, despite being a real improvement over Rounds 1 and 2 (no scale, no vibration), still produced "pushes down"/"appears instantly." `layout="position"` explicitly never animates size ("letting any size change snap instantly," per Motion's own docs) — but nothing in this file's editMode-toggle path ever animated real height either; React commits `targetHeight`/`targetMarginTop` to their full final values in one synchronous render regardless. So `layout="position"` is handed an already-full-size box and just a rect delta (old top-left vs. new top-left) to fake smoothness with, via a translate that starts the box at the *old* rect's position and eases toward the *new* one.

**Measured directly, not just reasoned through: `getComputedStyle` sampled every ~8ms across a real open transition.** `cardHeight` snapped `80.5px → 256.5px` in a single frame. `transformY` then eased `169.9 → 0` over the remaining ~250ms. Since the box's *size* was already final from frame 1, translating its *position* by that much means its bottom edge swept through the same ~170px range before settling — confirmed: `cardBottom` ranged from 956.5px down to 786.7px (its correct resting value) over the transition, even though Row 2, riding inside that same box, stayed measurably fixed the entire time (`row2Top` constant at 724.2px, 0.0px spread) thanks to its *own*, independent `layout="position"` — Motion's projection system automatically has a nested layout element counter-track whatever its animating ancestor is doing, confirmed by watching both elements' `transform`s in lockstep (e.g. `cardRef +25.45` / Row 2 `-25.45` at the same instant, summing to zero, at every sampled frame). That mechanism worked correctly the whole time — it's *why* Row 2 itself was never actually the problem here. But nothing was doing the equivalent for `cardRef`'s own box, so the border/background visibly swung past the stable controls and slid back into place — the "pushes down, appears instantly" reports, precisely.

**The fix isn't a better transform, it's not faking a reveal at all.** The outer wrapper's `marginTop` and `dialogRef`'s `height`/`margin-bottom` now animate via a genuine CSS `transition` (`geometryTransitionTiming` in `MixerBar.tsx`) instead of committing instantly and relying on `layout="position"` to paper over the resulting gap. `layout="position"` was removed from both `cardRef` and Row 2 entirely — Row 2 no longer needs to counter-track anything, because there's no longer an ambient transform on `cardRef` to counter-track in the first place.

**Why this is provably correct at every frame, not just the two endpoints, not merely observed to look right:** `targetMarginTop` and `targetHeight` are both derived from the same `effectiveHeightPx`, and their sum already cancels to a constant at any settled state (the existing marginTop-cancels-height identity this file has relied on since before this Motion migration began — see `growableFixedOverheadPx`'s own comment in `MixerBar.tsx`). Both properties transition using the *exact same* CSS duration and timing-function (one shared `geometryTransitionTiming` string, applied to both). Because interpolation is linear in each property's own start/end values, and the sum is constant at both endpoints by construction, the sum is provably constant at every point in between too — regardless of how curved the shared easing is. Verified anyway, not just trusted: `cardBottom` and `row2Top` both sampled every frame through full open *and* close transitions, spread 0.01px in both directions (pure sub-pixel rounding noise), while `cardTop`/`cardHeight` genuinely, smoothly interpolated end to end.

**Deliberately CSS-transition-driven, not Motion's `animate()`, and this one detail matters.** A CSS transition is resolved by the browser against its own style-commit/paint timing; `animate()` starts from inside a `useEffect`, which runs one tick after commit. This file has already hit exactly that class of gap once — `effectiveGrowableFixedOverheadPx`'s own comment documents `layout="position"` animating toward an intermediate, later-discarded value because Motion's own measurement ran on every render invocation, not once per commit. Two CSS transitions sharing one timing string can't drift apart that way; a CSS transition and a JS-effect-triggered `animate()` call plausibly could. Given the stakes (this is the second time this exact file has had two independently-computed animations fight or desync), the lower-risk, provably-synchronized option won even though it meant hand-writing a small `NamedEase → cubic-bezier()` table (`NAMED_EASE_TO_CUBIC_BEZIER` in `MixerBar.tsx`) for the AnimationDebugPanel's tunable eases — exact for `linear`/`easeIn`/`easeOut`/`easeInOut`/all three `back*` variants (all genuinely cubic-bezier curves), a close published approximation for the three `circ*` variants (not cubic-bezier-representable at all, but debug-panel-only, never the shipped default).

**The outer wrapper deliberately stays untouched by any of this — still a plain `div`, no Motion involvement whatsoever, exactly as Round 3 left it.** Only a CSS `transition` property was added to its existing `marginTop` style. The Round 3-era defensive fix this element carries (splitting `position: sticky` off from any element that could ever carry an active `transform`, a real-device-only WebKit interaction that "can't be directly confirmed without a real device") is about the `transform` CSS property specifically, not "any Motion involvement" as a blanket category — but given that fix was itself written under real uncertainty, converting this element to `motion.div` for a `marginTop` motion-value binding was considered and rejected as unnecessary risk for zero benefit, when a plain CSS transition on a plain `div` achieves the identical result with no new touchpoint on the one element this file has already flagged as fragile in exactly this area.

**Live drag is unaffected by any of this change.** `handleResizeStart`/`handleMove`/`handleEnd` never set `isEditModeTransitioning`/`isSnapTransitioning`, so `geometryTransitionMs` already evaluates to `0` (an instant, no-op CSS transition) for a drag's entire duration regardless. (`dragCardTranslateY`, referenced here in an earlier draft of this entry as "the only thing driving the live-drag visual," no longer exists at all — see the "grip press jumped the whole card" entry, below, for why even a constant transform turned out to be the wrong tool.)

**The "cardRef's box shows its full pinned extent from the first frame of a live drag" characteristic, mentioned in an earlier draft of this entry as an unfixed asymmetry, is still the correct, intended shape.** The box being visually static (not sliding) while pinned to its full extent from frame 1 is a direct consequence of growableRef's own real-height pin (performance, not a choice this entry's own mechanism makes) — nothing about the box's own position or size changes during the gesture, on purpose. What still doesn't happen live, and remains a real, separate, lower-priority gap: `TagPoolBody`'s own chip content doesn't visually grow to fill the revealed window during the drag itself — see the entry below for the fullest, most current account of this mechanism, since it changed again after this entry was first written.

**Verification methodology, for reference:** all of the above was measured, not assumed — `getComputedStyle().transform` and `getBoundingClientRect()` sampled at ~8-16ms intervals through live drag gestures and full open/close transitions via Playwright + CDP, the same battery of techniques this file's other MixerBar entries already established. Scripts live in this session's scratchpad, not checked into the repo.

## Grip press jumped the whole card, even the "fixed" per-drag-constant transform turned out to be the wrong tool — and a document-wide scroll lock was blocking scroll everywhere, not just on the panel

Two more reports, right after the fixes above landed. Open/close and the resize-shortcut arrows were confirmed working correctly. But: "clicking/holding the resize grip shifts the ENTIRE card (edit panel + controls) upward, and moving while engaged drags the whole card structure up/down... the resize grip should ONLY resize the edit panel... with the controls and card bottom staying pinned" — and separately, "now the ENTIRE view is frozen: when the panel is open, the user should still be able to scroll the page behind it when their cursor is OUTSIDE the card/edit panel. Only scrolling should be blocked when the cursor is WITHIN the card/edit panel."

### The grip press jump: a constant transform is still a transform, applied all at once, with no transition

The immediately-preceding entry's own fix (making `dragCardTranslateY` a per-drag *constant* instead of a per-tick value) was verified at the time by sampling `cardBottom` *during* an already-in-progress drag — and that sampling was completely correct, 0.0px drift confirmed. What it never checked was the single instant *before* the drag: a true before/after comparison bracketing `handleResizeStart` itself, not just samples taken after it had already run. Built that check directly (`page.mouse.down()` fires the handler synchronously, so any script that measures "before" only after calling it is already contaminated): pressing the grip, with zero pointer movement, jumped `cardBottom` and `row2Top` by **241px** in the single commit `handleResizeStart` runs in. The reported symptom and the actual mechanism were the same thing — a constant, non-zero `translateY` applied in one commit is still a discontinuous jump, just a jump to a fixed place instead of a continuously sliding one.

**The fix is the same shape as the open/close fix two entries up, applied to a value that had been overlooked as needing it: stop faking a correction with a transform, make the real geometry correct instead.** `targetMarginTop` (the outer wrapper's real, CSS-transitioned-but-instant-during-a-drag margin) now reads `poolPanelMaxHeight` instead of `effectiveHeightPx` whenever `dragHeightPx !== null` — in the exact same React commit that `handleResizeStart` pins `growableRef`/`dialogRef`'s real height to that same ceiling. `dragCardTranslateY` — the motion value, its seeding in `handleResizeStart`, its reset in `handleEnd`, and `cardRef`'s own `style={{ y: ... }}` — is deleted entirely, not just zeroed out. `cardRef` carries no transform of any kind for the live-drag case anymore, matching the open/close case exactly: nothing to fake, because nothing is wrong to begin with.

**A second, smaller bug turned up in the same investigation, once the fix above brought the jump down from 241px to a much-improved-but-still-measurable 16px.** The new `targetMarginTop` formula still added `effectiveGrowableFixedOverheadPx` (the measured `growableRef`-height-minus-pool-panel overhead, needed for the settled/non-drag case, where it genuinely passes through via ordinary flow-root margin collapse) even during a drag. But during a drag, `growableRef` also carries an explicit `overflow: hidden` alongside its pinned `height` — its own measured height is capped at *exactly* `poolPanelMaxHeight`, confirmed directly via `getBoundingClientRect` (590.3px, not 590.3 + 16). `dialogRef`'s own `marginBottom` past that point is clipped away by `growableRef`'s own overflow, not included in what it contributes to layout. Adding the overhead term on top of `poolPanelMaxHeight` during a drag over-compensated by exactly that overhead. Fixed: during a drag, `targetMarginTop` is just `-poolPanelMaxHeight`, no overhead term at all.

**Verified end to end, not just at the one point that was missed the first time:** a true before/after-press comparison (`cardBottom`/`row2Top` delta now `0.00`, only `cardTop` moves, reflecting the box's own real, pinned-and-capped extent — see the previous entry's own note on why that's the correct, accepted shape, not a bug), the full 0-150px range of an in-progress drag (constant throughout, re-confirmed), and release (settles cleanly to the new persisted height). `cardBottom = -(effectiveGrowableFixedOverheadPx + poolPanelMaxHeight) + poolPanelMaxHeight` algebraically confirmed to cancel to a constant independent of any drag state, and now actually matches what `growableRef` really measures at every step of that algebra, not just at the two ends.

### The document-wide scroll lock was never actually scoped to the panel, despite its own name

`html.mixbar-panel-scroll-locked` (`overflow: hidden` + `touch-action: none` on `<html>`, toggled for as long as `editMode` was open) was added specifically to fix a real-device report of the page and panel scrolling together as one unit. Reported now as itself too broad: legitimate page scroll, with the cursor nowhere near the panel, stopped working entirely whenever the panel was open.

**Both properties turned out to be genuinely document-wide at that scope, not scoped to "the panel" the way the class name implied.** `overflow: hidden` on `<html>` blocks wheel-driven scroll of the *document* outright — it's a property of the scrolling element being locked, with no concept of "unless the wheel event's target was over there instead." `touch-action` is a normal, inherited CSS property (same inheritance model as `color`), so `touch-action: none` on `<html>` becomes the *computed* value for any element on the page that doesn't set its own explicit override — not just the mixbar, every other element too, unless it happens to already carry its own touch-action (the way each `ScrollFadeArea` viewport does). "Resolves from where a touch starts" is true, but doesn't mean "scoped to the bar" when the bar is the only element *with* an override and the rest of the page just inherits the document's own `none`.

**Removed entirely — both the CSS class (`globals.css`) and its toggle effect (`MixerBar.tsx`).** What's left, already correctly scoped and untouched by this change: `blockScrollThrough` (an event listener attached directly to `cardRef`, so only wheel/touchmove events whose target is `cardRef` or a descendant ever reach it at all — no CSS inheritance involved, no document-wide side effect possible by construction) and the outer sticky wrapper's own `touch-none` class (a CSS property on one specific, tightly-scoped element, not the document root). Verified directly, both directions, on a page forced tall enough to have real scroll room: a wheel gesture with the cursor outside the card scrolls the page normally while the panel is open (`scrollY` 0 → 300); the identical gesture with the cursor over a blank area *inside* the card is fully absorbed, `scrollY` staying at `0`; a wheel gesture over an actual internal scroll area (the chip grid) still scrolls that area without the page moving.

**Whether this fully addresses the original real-device report is not yet confirmed, and shouldn't be assumed — flagged for on-device re-verification, not silently treated as settled.** That report predates essentially all of this session's compositor/transform-position work (the open/close fix, the live-drag fix, and the grip-press fix immediately above) — "the panel visibly scrolling even though its own size/position never changed" describes exactly the class of symptom those fixes exist to eliminate, so there's a real chance the original root cause is already gone. But that's a plausible hypothesis, not a measurement — this environment cannot reproduce the original bug to confirm either way, matching this file's own established real-device-only bug class. Needs confirmation on the same physical device the original report came from.

## Round 5: the live-drag "pin to a ceiling, fake the reveal with a clip-path" mechanism was itself the bug — real geometry tracks the pointer directly again, and it turns out to be just as fast

The grip-press fix (immediately above) made cardRef's own box stop jumping and sliding — verified, precisely, at 0.00px drift on press, throughout a drag, and at release. Reported immediately after, just as precisely: "the resize affordance cause the panel to fully expand on click then on let go reverts to the size it was at... does not resize as expected." Both were true at once, because they were never in tension — one was about *whether the box moves when it shouldn't*, the other about *whether it visibly changes size when it should*, and Round 4's whole mechanism had gotten the first one right while never actually addressing the second.

### What "pin to poolPanelMaxHeight, reveal via clip-path" actually looked like to a user

The live-drag performance fix (two entries up) pinned `growableRef`/`dialogRef`'s *real* height to `poolPanelMaxHeight` once, at drag start, and faked the visible growth with a `clip-path` motion value tracking the pointer. That clip-path really was tracking the pointer correctly, the whole time — confirmed directly, comparing its computed `inset()` value against the pointer position at multiple points through a drag, exact match every time. But `cardRef`'s own real height (the border, background, shadow — the *only* thing a user actually reads as "how big is this panel") is derived from `growableRef`'s real, **pinned** height, via ordinary block flow — not from the clip-path, which only ever affected what was *painted inside* `growableRef`, never `cardRef`'s own box. The border was at its full, maximum possible extent from the very first frame of every drag, completely independent of where the invisible clip boundary actually was. There is nothing in this design that visually marks that boundary — no seam, no color change, uniform background throughout — so to a user, the whole panel reads as "already fully expanded, unmoving" for the entire drag, and then, on release, when the real height finally snaps down to wherever the pointer actually was, reads as "reverting." An earlier draft of the previous entry called this static full-extent border "a known, accepted tradeoff." That assessment was wrong — it wasn't a minor asymmetry, it was the entire bug, just never connected to the report because the measurements taken at the time (`cardBottom`/`row2Top` drift) were correctly checking for accidental *movement*, not checking whether the *right* thing was moving at all.

### The fix: stop pinning to a ceiling, track the real value directly — and re-measure whether that's still fast enough, don't assume

This is a straightforward reversion, in spirit, back to the pre-Motion-migration behavior: `growableRef`/`dialogRef`'s real height and the outer wrapper's real `marginTop` now track the live drag value (`next`) directly, every tick, exactly as they would if there were no drag-specific optimization at all. What's different from a literal revert is *how* they track it — via Motion values (`dragLiveHeight`, `dragMarginTop`, both new; `dragRevealHeight`/`dragClipPath`, both from the pin+clip-path design, removed entirely), `.set()` every tick, bypassing React's render cycle. `growableRef`/`dialogRef` both went back to being exactly what they were before any drag-specific work touched them — `growableRef` a plain, unstyled `flow-root` `<div>` again, `dialogRef` a `motion.div` now (was a plain `<div>`) purely so its `height` can bind to `dragLiveHeight` during a drag, `targetHeight` otherwise, still carrying no `layout` prop of any kind. The outer sticky wrapper became a `motion.div` too, for the identical reason on `marginTop` — the sticky+transform WebKit concern that kept it a plain `<div>` through Round 4 (see that entry) is specifically about the `transform` CSS property; `margin-top` isn't one, so it doesn't apply here.

This is a **real** layout property changing on every tick again — exactly what the original measurement this whole line of work started from (60 real layout passes for 60 ticks, zero amortization) was measuring the cost of. Re-measured directly before committing to this reversion, not assumed safe just because it's simpler: `Performance.getMetrics` still shows 60 real `LayoutCount` increments for 60 ticks (unsurprising — `contain: layout` isolates the *cost* of a descendant's own layout work, it doesn't change *whether* a layout pass happens for the element whose own size actually changed). What matters is whether each of those 60 passes is now cheap enough to fit inside a frame, and it is: the same CPU-throttled (6x) frame-gap sampling this session already established as the real-world-relevant metric showed **0 dropped frames out of 90, worst gap 12.8ms** — matching the pin+clip-path design's own result exactly, with a mechanism that's both simpler and doesn't have a class of bug this session had to find twice. `contain: layout` on `dialogRef` and `pauseMeasurementWhileResizing` on the chip area's own `ScrollFadeArea` (both landed as part of the original performance work, alongside the pin+clip-path mechanism itself) turned out to be doing the real work all along — the pin+clip-path indirection on top of them wasn't buying anything a real user could perceive as smoother, and cost a resize handle that didn't visibly resize.

**The general lesson, worth stating plainly since it very nearly went the other way under time pressure: a raw pass-count metric (LayoutCount) and a perceptible-jank metric (dropped frames under realistic throttling) can diverge once other, unrelated optimizations change the cost of each pass — measure the metric that actually maps to what a user experiences, not the one that's easiest to drive to zero.** Chasing LayoutCount to its minimum (1 pass per drag, via pinning) produced a mechanism that was fast by that metric and broken by every metric that actually mattered to a user holding the grip down.

**Verified end to end:** press causes zero movement of any kind now, not even `cardTop` (there's no pin to compensate for anymore — the real height starts at exactly `startHeight` and grows from there). The full existing drag verification suite (`verify-mixerbar-drag.mjs`) passes outright now, including an assertion — "dialog height tracks the pointer 1:1" — that had been *failing* under the pin+clip-path design (correctly, since that design deliberately didn't track the pointer in real terms) and is passing again now, back to checking real behavior instead of a since-removed one. Row 2/the controls remain at 0.00px drift throughout press, drag, and release — that part of Round 4's own fix needed no changes at all, since nothing about how `cardRef`'s own bottom edge stays anchored (the outer wrapper's `marginTop` canceling `growableRef`'s real height, the same algebraic identity this file has relied on since before any of this session's work) changed; only *how* the live value reaches that formula did. No orphaned-motion-value-style risk either, re-checked directly: unlike the conditionally-*present*-key pattern that caused the stale clip-path bug (Round 4 entry, "grip press jumped the whole card"), `dialogRef`/the outer wrapper's `height`/`marginTop` keys are now always present, only ever switching *which kind* of value they hold (a Motion value during a drag, a plain number otherwise) — confirmed live that this pattern does not leave a stale value behind the way the other one did.

## The mobile-only open/close/resize jitter: `cardRef` had no containment boundary, so Row 2 was being re-touched every single frame for no reason

Reported throughout this session as real-device-only — never reproduced in headless Chromium, an emulated-mobile viewport, or CDP CPU throttling up to 20x, matching this file's own established pattern for this bug class (see the scroll-through and sticky+transform entries above). Diagnosed this time from actual on-device evidence rather than another round of guessing: the user captured Safari Remote Web Inspector Timelines and Elements-panel snapshots directly from their physical iPhone during a live resize.

**What the capture showed, read directly rather than summarized:** the Timeline's Layout & Rendering track showed continuous, repeated Styles-Recalculated → Layout-Invalidated → Layout → Composite → Paint cycles for the entire ~15s window of touch activity, CPU sustained in the 60-80% range throughout. Two Elements-panel snapshots, taken seconds apart mid-resize, showed the outer sticky wrapper's real `margin-top` at two different values (-215px, then -261px) and `dialogRef`'s real `height` at two different values (199px, then 245px) — both moving together, confirming the capture was mid-drag under this file's own real-time-tracking mechanism (previous entry). The user then deliberately expanded every rendered element in the paint-flashing test rather than trusting an earlier, narrower pass, and found one they'd missed: the tag-slots row's own "Scroll right" button — a descendant of Row 2 (the controls row), with nothing to do with the edit panel — showing signs of being touched on every frame too.

**Root cause: `contain: layout` existed on exactly one element, `dialogRef`, two levels inside the edit panel — and it only isolates `dialogRef`'s *own* descendants.** `cardRef` (the actual bordered card, direct parent of both the edit panel and Row 2) carried no containment of its own. When the outer wrapper's real `margin-top` changes — every frame, by design, per the live-drag and open/close mechanisms both — nothing tells the browser it can treat `cardRef`'s subtree as a sealed, already-computed unit that simply shifts. It has to walk back into everything inside `cardRef`, Row 2 included, to confirm nothing there needs to change, every single tick, even though the answer is always "nothing." `dialogRef`'s own containment never had any bearing on this: it protects its own descendants from the outside world, not its uncle.

This resolves two things at once. **Why it's real-device-only**: the unnecessary re-walk of Row 2 always completes well inside a frame on anything fast enough — desktop, headless, CDP-throttled up to 20x, all showed 0 or near-0 dropped frames. A real phone doesn't always finish that wasted work in time, and that reads as jitter — not a wrong answer arriving, a correct one arriving late. **Why bisecting the jitter back through git history (as far as commits from well before this session's Motion migration) never found a clean commit**: `cardRef` (or its equivalent ancestor in every earlier version of this file) has animated `margin-top`/`height` every frame since this bar was first built, and none of those versions ever had a containment boundary around it either. The gap predates everything this session touched; there was nothing to bisect.

**Fix: added `[contain:layout]` to `cardRef`'s own className** — `layout` alone, matching `dialogRef`'s own existing choice, not `layout paint` or `strict`, since only layout isolation is needed here (nothing relies on `cardRef` clipping overflow or gaining a new stacking/paint boundary).

**Verification status, stated plainly rather than overclaimed:** every positional-correctness check (press, drag, release, open, close) re-run after this change shows results byte-identical to before it — 0.00px drift throughout, confirming the fix is not just theoretically inert but measurably so. The CPU-throttle frame-drop test does **not** show a clean win, and honesty about that matters more than a tidy before/after story: at 20x throttle (well past the 6x this file otherwise uses), five runs without the fix averaged ~34 dropped frames (28-40 range), five runs with it averaged ~39 (33-45 range) — noisy, overlapping, arguably trending the wrong direction, and not something either number should be trusted over the other at this sample size. This doesn't undermine the fix: CDP's CPU throttle slows down JS/main-thread execution uniformly, which is a different kind of bottleneck than whatever a real phone's GPU/compositor is actually hitting, and this bug has never once reproduced under any desktop proxy tried across this entire investigation. The fix is justified directly by the on-device capture (a concrete, real element being touched every frame for no reason, now structurally prevented), not by a desktop metric that was never able to see this bug in the first place. Needs on-device re-confirmation before being treated as resolved, same as every other real-device-only fix in this file.

## Round 6: the jitter's real remaining cost was `dialogRef`'s own real height changing every frame, not just `cardRef`'s missing containment — pin during a live drag again, track the real value only for the discrete cases

The `[contain:layout]` fix above was real and independently justified (a concrete, on-device-confirmed cost, structurally removed) but the user's own on-device retest after it shipped still showed the jitter. Their own analysis, from a screenshot showing `dialogRef`'s real `height` reading two different values seconds apart mid-drag, named the mechanism directly: containment isolates an element's descendants from *ancestor*-triggered re-evaluation, it does nothing about `dialogRef`'s own real height changing on every tick being a genuine, unavoidable-by-containment layout cost in its own right. Round 5 traded the pin+clip-path mechanism's one real bug (the border decoupled from the clip) for exactly the per-frame real-layout-write cost this whole line of work started out trying to eliminate, and `contain: layout` alone was never going to make a real, per-tick `height` write free.

The two needs are genuinely different interactions, not one problem in two disguises: the live pointer-drag needs cheap, compositor-only tracking every tick (60fps, sustained, on a phone); the discrete open/close/50%/100%/reset cases need real geometry, but only for a short, bounded CSS transition (~17 layout passes over 280ms, never shown to be a cost problem).
