Round 4's mistake wasn't pinning during a drag — it was pinning to a ceiling and clip-path-revealing on the *wrong element*. `growableRef`, where that clip-path lived, is a descendant of `cardRef`; `cardRef`'s own real height (border, background, shadow — the only thing a user reads as "how big is this panel") derives from `growableRef`'s pinned real height via ordinary block flow, completely independent of what `growableRef`'s own clip-path was doing to `growableRef`'s own content.

**Fix: pin `dialogRef`'s real height (and, by flow, `growableRef`'s and `cardRef`'s) to `poolPanelMaxHeight` for a live drag's whole duration again, same as Round 4 — but put the clip-path on `cardRef` itself this time, not `growableRef`.** `cardRef` is the one element that owns the border, so a clip-path there clips the border along with everything else: what's painted matches the live clip boundary at every frame, not a value decoupled from it. `clip-path: inset(topPx 0 0 0)`, top-only (growth is always upward, the bottom edge is the fixed anchor `dragMarginTop`'s own algebra is already built around), where `topPx = poolPanelMaxHeight - next`. `cardRef`'s own padding/border/Row 2 are all constant terms that cancel out of that difference algebraically, so the formula is correct regardless of `cardRef`'s own box model, not a number that happens to work at one specific height. `dragMarginTop` still cancels whatever height is currently pinned, via the same M+G=0 identity this file has used since long before any of these rounds — that identity holds for *any* height value, which is what makes pinning to a ceiling larger than the current visual size safe (no jump for Row 2/controls), the exact guarantee Round 3's constant-transform attempt didn't have.

`flushPendingMove` (the rAF-throttled per-tick handler) now touches exactly one thing per tick: the clip-path Motion value. `dragPinnedHeight`/`dragMarginTop` are set once, at drag start, and once again at release (back to the real final value) — not per tick. `pinScrollToDocumentBottomIfWasAtBottom()`'s own per-tick call (added under Round 5, when real height genuinely changed every tick) is gone too, for the same reason: the document's real `scrollHeight` now only actually changes twice during a drag (once when the pin is applied, once when it's released), not on every tick in between.

**Verified, not assumed:** a Playwright script pressed the grip with zero pointer movement (0.00px visual jump, accounting for the clip-path offset — `getBoundingClientRect()` alone is blind to a clip-path and will misreport a pinned-but-clipped element as having jumped when it hasn't), sampled the visually-clipped position continuously through an 8-step drag (bottom edge fixed at 0.00px drift throughout; height increasing smoothly and monotonically, not frozen-then-snapped), and released (0.00px jump against the last drag sample, settled state identical to the immediate post-release state). The discrete 100%-snap shortcut, a fully separate code path, was re-checked and unaffected. One shadow concern flagged before implementing, not after: `clip-path` also clips `box-shadow`, so `cardRef`'s hard offset shadow (`5px 5px 0`, no blur) could in principle visibly truncate at the live clip boundary. Checked directly via screenshot during a simulated live drag rather than assumed either way: this specific shadow style is offset down-and-right only, so it never paints above or left of the border box to begin with, and the clip here only ever removes from the *top* — no visible artifact, confirmed, not just reasoned about. **This specific conclusion was wrong — see the entry directly below.** The screenshot check at the time only looked at the top edge; it never checked whether the shadow was still *present*, only whether the top edge looked clean. It wasn't present. Left here rather than silently corrected, same reason every other wrong-turn in this file stays on the record.

Needs on-device re-confirmation before being treated as resolved, same as every other real-device-only fix in this file — this is the fourth attempt at the same reported symptom (Rounds 3, 4, 5, and this one), and the first three all looked correct by every check available off-device too.

**On-device confirmation came back positive for the jitter itself** — the pin-during-drag mechanism above is confirmed fixed on a real phone. Three rendering side-effects of it were reported alongside that confirmation, all traced to the clip-path sitting on `cardRef`: the shadow vanishing during a drag (the mistake corrected above), a roughly 1px compression of Row 2's controls at press, and the panel showing blank/white space during a drag instead of real content at the newly-revealed size. Two of the three are fixed below; the third is flagged honestly as still open.

**Shadow: `clip-path` clips an element's entire paint output uniformly — border, background, content, AND box-shadow — regardless of which direction the shadow is offset.** The corrected entry above was wrong for exactly the reason its own correction says: it checked whether the border looked clean, not whether the shadow was still there. A tempting first fix — move the clip-path one level in, onto `growableRef` (which wraps only the growable content, already a sibling of Row 2, carries no border/shadow of its own) — was tried and **confirmed live to reintroduce Round 4's exact bug**: a child's `clip-path` cannot shrink its parent's own box. With `growableRef` clipped and `cardRef` left with no clip-path of its own, `cardRef`'s own border rendered at its full *pinned* extent for the whole drag, unclipped by anything — measured directly, `cardRef`'s own `rawTop` jumped ~310px on press and sat frozen there through an entire drag, snapping down only at release. `cardRef` has to be the clipped element for its own border to track the drag at all; this is not optional, and the fix couldn't be "clip somewhere else instead."

**Fix: move the shadow off `cardRef`'s `box-shadow` entirely, onto `filter: drop-shadow(...)` on the *outer sticky wrapper* — an ancestor of `cardRef` that is never itself clipped.** A `filter` on an element applies to that element's already-rendered subtree, clip-path included — by the time the outer wrapper's own filter runs, `cardRef`'s clip-path has already been applied, so `drop-shadow` tracks `cardRef`'s current *clipped* (correct, live) shape instead of an unclipped one the way `box-shadow` on `cardRef` itself couldn't. `--elev-4`'s own values (`5px 5px 0 var(--ink)`) map directly, no blur radius either way, so this is a cheap offset+composite recompute per tick, not a gaussian blur. Verified directly: a tight, high-DPI screenshot of the bottom-right corner (where both the x and y offset combine, the most visible point) taken at rest and mid-drag are pixel-identical — the shadow sliver renders at the same relative position to the border in both.

**White content: separate root cause, separate fix.** `TagPoolBody`'s own content is deliberately frozen at drag-start size for a live drag's whole duration — its `poolPanelHeightPx` prop comes from `dragHeightPx`, which (like `dragPinnedHeight`) is only ever set at press and release, never per tick, specifically to avoid re-triggering `TagPoolBody`'s own `ResizeObserver`-driven remeasurement on every tick (a real, already-fixed jitter contributor — see `pauseMeasurementWhileResizing`, above). That's an existing, deliberate tradeoff, not something this round introduced. What Round 6 changed is that the *outer frame* now visibly grows past that frozen content via the clip-path reveal — and `dialogRef`'s default `justify-start` (its only child, `TagPoolBody`, anchored to `dialogRef`'s own top) left a growing gap between the frozen content's own bottom edge and `dialogRef`'s real (pinned, growing) bottom edge. Both `dialogRef` and `TagPoolBody`'s root are `background: transparent`, so that gap showed `cardRef`'s own bare background straight through — measured directly on a 400px drag: `TagPoolBody`'s root stayed exactly 160px tall while `dialogRef` grew to 470px, leaving 310px of see-through gap.

Fix: `justify-end` on `dialogRef`, **conditional on `dragHeightPx !== null`, not unconditional.** `growableRef`'s clip-path always reveals `cardRef`'s bottom `next` px, anchored at the true (fixed) bottom — flush-ending the frozen `TagPoolBody` against that same bottom edge means growing the drag target can never open a gap (the revealed window and the frozen content always share a bottom edge, and the window is at least as tall as the content once `next >= startHeight`), and shrinking correctly clips `TagPoolBody` from the top instead, which is the direction a shrinking panel should read. Made conditional specifically because the discrete open/close CSS transition relies on the *opposite* default: content taller than the mid-transition box, revealing top-down as `dialogRef` grows — flipping that anchor unconditionally would have changed that animation's own direction as a side effect, a regression this fix has no business introducing. Verified directly: at the same 400px drag, `TagPoolBody`'s root now sits flush against `dialogRef`'s bottom edge, unchanged from its rest position, with real chips visibly rendered at the bottom of the revealed area instead of blank space.

**1px compression: not resolved, reported honestly rather than guessed at.** The working theory was that Row 2, as a *descendant* of `cardRef` while `cardRef` carries an active clip-path, might be pulled into a compositing/rasterization boundary the clip creates, causing a subpixel snap — even though Row 2 itself is never geometrically clipped (it always sits in the revealed region). Moving the clip-path off `cardRef` would have tested that theory directly, but that's exactly the move that broke border-tracking and had to be reverted — so this fix ships with `cardRef`'s clip-path in the identical location it was in when the 1px compression was first reported. Measured the resize grip's own `getBoundingClientRect()` at rest/press/mid-drag/release and found it byte-identical (no sub-pixel difference at all) through press and the whole drag — but `getBoundingClientRect()` reports layout geometry, not painted pixels, and a compositing snap is exactly the kind of artifact that wouldn't show up there (clip-path itself is invisible to this same measurement, which is why the drag-tracking checks above compute a separate "visual" position). This needs a real-device, real-eyes re-check specifically — not claimed fixed, not claimed still-broken, genuinely unknown from here.

## Path B (real height, no clip-path) + the drag-time cost is COMPOSITE, not layout: kill the growing-layer filter

Rounds 3-6 above are superseded on branch `diag/mixbar-resize-animation`. The live resize now animates the pool panel's **real height** through the ordinary `effectiveHeightPx → targetHeight`/`targetMarginTop` pipeline, fed live: `handleMove` writes `dragHeightPx` (React state) every rAF tick, and `dialogRef`'s `height` + the outer wrapper's `marginTop` are plain React inline styles committed together in one render. No pinned ceiling, no clip-path, no separate "visual" value. This fixed the star clip (symptom 3 — `RecommendButton`'s star bleeds outside the card as it always did, because there's no clip-path to cut it) and the content-lag (symptom 2 — the chip area is laid out at the real live height every tick, so it reflows continuously), both confirmed on-device.

**A single-source `useMotionValue` variant ("Path C") was built and reverted.** The theory was that the jitter came from `height` (on `dialogRef`) and `margin-top` (on the outer wrapper, three DOM levels up) landing in two different WebKit paints, and that driving both from one motion value + a `useTransform` would make them atomic. Two things killed it: (1) the Motion docs confirm a motion value and its `useTransform` derivatives are **already batched to one animation frame** — React's own single-commit inline styles were never split either, so there was no two-paint gap to close, and the on-device jitter persisted unchanged; (2) the change required the chip area to CSS-flex-fill the motion-value-driven dialog (a deep `flex-1 min-h-0` chain), which iOS WebKit resolves unreliably — it stretched the whole card past its viewport at some breakpoints (never reproduced in desktop Chrome at any width, the same real-device-only pattern as everything else in this file). Reverted wholesale back to Path B.

**The actual diagnosis came from an on-device Safari Timeline capture of a single Layout event mid-drag, and it inverts everything above.** Read directly: `Layout` = 0.153ms (the panel box is 390×699 and laying it out is free), `Styles Recalculated` ≈ 0.6ms, but `Composite` = 1.545 → 2.239 → 2.905ms and **climbing across the drag**. The bottleneck was never layout — it's compositing, and a composite cost that *grows* as the panel grows is a compositor layer being repainted and re-uploaded at an ever-larger texture size every frame. This also retroactively explains why every real-height round jittered on-device while Round 6's clip-path (which pinned the real height and never grew a real box) did not: pinning avoided the growing layer, it didn't avoid a layout cost that was always negligible.

**Fix 1 — the growing layer was the shadow.** The card's shadow had been moved from `cardRef`'s own `box-shadow` to a `filter: drop-shadow(5px 5px 0 var(--ink))` on the outer sticky wrapper back in Round 6, specifically so it would track `cardRef`'s live clip-path (a filter on an ancestor applies after the descendant's clip). Path B deleted the clip-path, so that reason was already gone — but the filter stayed. A `filter: drop-shadow` promotes its whole subtree to a compositor layer and re-renders that layer (the filter is a GPU pass over the element's rasterised output) every frame the subtree's size changes — i.e. the entire drag, at a texture that grows with the panel. That is exactly the climbing composite curve. Moved the shadow back to `cardRef`'s own `box-shadow: var(--elev-4)` (the pre-Round-6 home): a `0`-blur offset shadow paints inline with the border in the ordinary paint pass, no filter layer, no per-frame re-upload, so composite cost stops scaling with panel height. Visually identical (same `5px 5px 0 --ink` hard shadow), and `contain: layout` (not `paint`) on `cardRef` doesn't clip it. Verified on desktop: `getComputedStyle(cardRef).boxShadow` shows the shadow, `card.filter`/`wrapper.filter` are both `none`, screenshot mid-drag shows the shadow rendering correctly and the star still bleeding un-clipped.

**Fix 2 — the mixbar's four structural `motion.div`s were vestigial.** The outer wrapper, `cardRef`, `dialogRef`, and Row 2's container were all `motion.div` left over from the Round 1-4 FLIP migration, but Path B drives them entirely with plain React inline styles — no `layout` prop, no motion value, no `animate`. A `motion` component still spins up a `VisualElement` + projection node with per-frame work (the repeating minified `tJ` in the Timeline's Initiator column) and Motion's own layer-promotion (the MotionScore audit's long-standing "stale `will-change` on 1 layer" flag — there is no `will-change` anywhere in this project's own source). Converted all four to plain `div`, which drops `motion/react` from `MixerBar` entirely. The mixbar's Motion migration is effectively fully unwound now: real geometry via CSS/React, no Motion in the resize path at all.

Both fixes target composite specifically and need on-device re-measurement of the same Timeline to confirm the curve flattens — desktop composite is cheap everywhere and cannot show the win. A separate, still-unshipped idea if residual sub-pixel vibration remains after the composite fix: the drag-time `marginTop` uses a `getBoundingClientRect()`-measured overhead (fractional), while `growableRef` renders at exactly `height + DIALOG_MARGIN_BOTTOM_PX`, so the `16 − measuredOverhead` residual shifts Row 2 by a sub-pixel that changes every frame; using the exact `DIALOG_MARGIN_BOTTOM_PX` constant during a drag (as the open/close toggle frame already does) makes the compensation pixel-exact. Held back deliberately so the composite fix can be measured in isolation.
